Final Mock Exam 2 (formulas)

1. Normal equation: θ = (XᵀX + λI)⁻¹Xᵀy. We have m = 120 patients and n = 4 input variables (with the dummy column x₀ = 1). Which statements are true?Lecture 4
A. XᵀX is a 5×5 matrix.
B. Xᵀy has 5 elements.
C. I is a 120×120 identity matrix.
D. With λ = 0, we get the non-regularized normal equation.
E. θ has 4 elements.
F. y has 120 elements.
2. MSE loss: J(Θ) = 1/(2m) Σⱼ₌₁ᵐ (ŷ⁽ʲ⁾ − y⁽ʲ⁾)². We have m = 3, ŷ = [2, 4, 6], y = [1, 5, 9]. Which statements are true?Lecture 2
A. J = 11/6.
B. The sum of the squared errors is 11.
C. If the factor 1/(2m) were replaced by 1/m, the loss would be 11/3.
D. J = 11/3.
E. J can be negative.
F. ∂J/∂ŷ⁽¹⁾ = (ŷ⁽¹⁾ − y⁽¹⁾)/m = 1/3.
3. Multi-output regression: J(Θ) = 1/(2mk) Σⱼ₌₁ᵐ Σᵢ₌₁ᵏ (ŷᵢ⁽ʲ⁾ − yᵢ⁽ʲ⁾)². We have m = 2 examples and k = 2 outputs. ŷ = [[1, 2], [3, 4]] and y = [[1, 0], [1, 4]]. Which statements are true?Lecture 2
A. J = 1.
B. The sum of all squared errors is 8.
C. The denominator 2mk is 8.
D. J = 2.
E. The loss is averaged over the elements of the label vector too.
F. J is a vector with 2 elements.
4. Binary cross-entropy for one example: L = −[y·ln ŷ + (1 − y)·ln(1 − ŷ)]. (ln 0.8 ≈ −0.22, ln 0.5 ≈ −0.69.) Which statements are true?Lecture 3
A. For y = 1 and ŷ = 0.8, L ≈ 0.22.
B. For y = 0 and ŷ = 0.2, L ≈ 0.22.
C. For y = 1 and ŷ = 0.5, L ≈ 0.69.
D. For y = 1 and ŷ → 0, L → ∞.
E. For y = 0, only the term y·ln ŷ matters.
F. With logistic regression, the BCE loss is convex.
5. Cross-entropy for multi-class classification: L = −Σᵢ yᵢ ln ŷᵢ. The true label is one-hot y = [0, 0, 1], and the estimate is ŷ = [0.1, 0.2, 0.7]. (ln 0.7 ≈ −0.36, ln 3 ≈ 1.10.) Which statements are true?Lecture 5
A. L ≈ 0.36.
B. Only the third term of the sum is non-zero.
C. If ŷ = [1/3, 1/3, 1/3], then L ≈ 1.10.
D. The elements of ŷ should sum to 1 (softmax output).
E. The elements of y are the softmax outputs.
6. Two-layer MLP: h(x) = g₂(W₂ g₁(W₁x + b₁) + b₂). The input has n = 10 variables, the hidden layer has 6 neurons, and the output has k = 3 classes (multi-class classification). Which statements are true?Lecture 5
A. W₁ ∈ ℝ^(6×10)
B. W₂ ∈ ℝ^(3×6)
C. b₂ ∈ ℝ³
D. The network has 87 parameters.
E. W₁x + b₁ ∈ ℝ¹⁰
F. g₂ should be the identity.
7. Gradient descent update: θ := θ − α·∂J/∂θ. Let J(θ) = (θ − 3)², with θ = 1. Which statements are true?Lecture 2
A. ∂J/∂θ = −4.
B. With α = 0.25, the new θ is 2.
C. With α = 0.25, J decreases from 4 to 1.
D. With α = 1.5, the new θ is 7, and J increases to 16.
E. The correct update rule is θ := θ + α·∂J/∂θ.
8. Sigmoid: f(x) = 1/(1 + e⁻ˣ). Which statements are true? (Use f(ln 3) = 0.75.)Lecture 5
A. f′(0) = 0.25.
B. f′(x) = f(x)(1 − f(x)).
C. f′(ln 3) = 3/16.
D. f′(x) = e⁻ˣ/(1 + e⁻ˣ)².
E. The maximum value of f′(x) is 1.
9. Backpropagation with J = ((wx + b) − y)². The graph is q = wx, ŷ = q + b, z = y − ŷ, J = z². Values: x = 1, y = 3, w = 2, b = −1. Which statements are true?Lecture 6
A. J = 4.
B. ∂J/∂b = −4.
C. ∂J/∂w = −4.
D. ∂J/∂x = −8.
E. After one step with α = 0.5: w = 4 and b = 1.
F. After this step, the loss is 0.
10. `yp = np.array([2, 4, 6])` and `y = np.array([1, 5, 9])`. Which statements are true?NumPy
A. `np.sum((yp - y) ** 2) == 11`
B. `np.mean((yp - y) ** 2) / 2` ≈ 1.83
C. `np.mean(yp - y) ** 2` equals 11/3
D. `(yp - y[:, None]).shape == (3, 3)`
E. `np.sum(yp > y) == 1`
F. `np.argmax(np.abs(yp - y)) == 2`
11. Normal equation in NumPy. `X` has shape (100, 4), `y` has shape (100,), and `lam = 0.1`. Which statements are true?NumPy
A. `(X.T @ X).shape == (4, 4)`
B. `(X.T @ y).shape == (4,)`
C. `np.linalg.inv(X.T @ X + lam * np.eye(4)).shape == (4, 4)`
D. `X.T @ X + lam * np.eye(100)` works and returns shape (100, 100).
E. `(np.linalg.inv(X.T @ X + lam * np.eye(4)) @ X.T @ y).shape == (4,)`
12. `z = np.array([0., np.log(3)])`, `h = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))`, `y = np.array([1, 0])`, `X = np.array([[1, 0], [1, 1]])`. Which statements are true?NumPy
A. `h` equals `[0.5, 0.75]`
B. `np.mean(-y * np.log(h) - (1 - y) * np.log(1 - h))` ≈ 1.04
C. `(X.T @ (h - y) / y.size).shape == (2,)`
D. `X.T @ (h - y) / y.size` equals `[0.125, 0.375]`
E. `h - y` equals `[0.5, 0.75]`
13. `X = np.array([[1., 10.], [2., 20.], [3., 30.], [4., 40.]])`. (`np.std` uses the population formula by default.) Which statements are true?NumPy
A. `X.mean(axis=0)` equals `[2.5, 25.]`
B. `X.std(axis=0)` ≈ `[1.12, 11.18]`
C. `((X - X.mean(axis=0)) / X.std(axis=0))[0]` ≈ `[-1.34, -1.34]`
D. `((X - X.min(axis=0)) / (X.max(axis=0) - X.min(axis=0)))[:, 0]` has 1/3 as its second element.
E. `X.mean(axis=1).shape == (2,)`
F. `X.max(axis=0) - X.min(axis=0)` equals `[3., 30.]`
14. Softmax in NumPy. `Z = np.array([[0, 0], [np.log(2), 0]])`, `e = np.exp(Z)`, `P = e / e.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)`. Which statements are true?NumPy
A. `P[1]` ≈ `[0.667, 0.333]`
B. `P.sum(axis=1)` equals `[1, 1]`
C. `e / e.sum(axis=1)` raises a ValueError.
D. `e.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True).shape == (2, 1)`
E. `np.argmax(P, axis=1)` equals `[0, 0]`
15. One gradient descent step for linear regression. `X = np.array([[1, 0], [1, 1], [1, 2]])`, `y = np.array([1, 3, 5])`, `theta = np.zeros(2)`, `lr = 0.1`, `grad = X.T @ (X @ theta - y) / y.size`. Which statements are true?NumPy
A. `grad` ≈ `[-3., -4.33]`
B. After `theta = theta - lr * grad`, theta ≈ `[0.3, 0.43]`
C. The initial loss `np.sum((X @ theta - y) ** 2) / (2 * y.size)` ≈ 5.83
D. `grad.shape == (2,)`
E. `theta = theta + lr * grad` would also decrease the loss.
F. `(X @ theta).shape == (3,)`

Answers and explanations

Mock Exam 1

1. N-13 — Answer: A, C, D, E
A true. B false: boolean indexing always flattens the result. C true. D true. E true.
2. N-05 — Answer: B, C, D, E
(32, 5) and (32,) conflict at the last axis (5 vs 32), so a ValueError is raised (A false, B true). B[:, None] is (32, 1), which expands along the columns -> (32, 5) (C true). A scalar is applied to every element (D true). Broadcasting compares the trailing dimensions first (E true).
3. L5-14 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: AND and OR are linearly separable and can be represented by a single neuron. ⚠️ Trap: E — "AND and OR cannot be represented" → "they can be represented" would make it TRUE.
4. N-27 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true (Python's and/or require a single truth value for the whole array). E false: the result is an array, not a scalar. ⚠️ Trap: E — "always a scalar bool" → "an element-wise bool array" would make it TRUE.
5. L4-11 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A simplified and discretized model of a biological neuron (A true). g = sigmoid → logistic regression (B true). Other activation functions are possible (C true). A building block of an MLP (D true). An artificial neuron computes a weighted sum plus an activation (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "does not compute a weighted sum" → "computes a weighted sum plus an activation" would make it TRUE.
6. L1-07 — Answer: A, B, C, E
Generalization: memorizing versus learning something useful (A true). Decision trees are interpretable but limited (B true). Adversarial attacks work without knowing the architecture or parameters, and they also work in reality (C true). Critical software can be formally verified, but deep learning can only be tested empirically (D false). Knowledge learned by neural networks cannot be explained well (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — "can be fully verified" → "is hard to verify" would make it TRUE.
7. N-04 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true (32-1=31). C true. D true. E false: [[0], :] is fancy indexing, which keeps the axis, giving (1, 5).
8. N-16 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true (-1 is computed as 12/3=4). B true. C true. D true (4 != 3). E false: * is element-wise and @ is matrix multiplication. ⚠️ Trap: E — "* and @ perform the same operation" → "they differ (element-wise vs matrix product)" would make it TRUE.
9. L4-14 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A complex model memorizes the training examples and overfits (A true). Logistic regression has a linear decision surface, so overfitting is not expected (B true). Lack of capacity → underfitting (C true). Balance is the goal (D true). Logistic regression can only make linear boundaries, so overfitting through complex boundaries is not expected (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "always prone to overfitting" → "not expected, since the boundary is linear" would make it TRUE.
10. L5-16 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true (argmax). C true. D true. E false: the sum of a softmax output is 1. ⚠️ Trap: E — "the sum equals the number of classes" → "the sum is 1" would make it TRUE.
11. L3-07 — Answer: A, B, C, E
2 features plus bias give 3 parameters (A true). X is (100, 3) (B true). X*theta is unbounded, g(X*theta) is in [0, 1] (C true). The minimized quantity is the loss J (D false, E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — "the sigmoid function g itself" → "the parameters theta" would make it TRUE.
12. N-32 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: it can easily be implemented with NumPy even without np.histogram. ⚠️ Trap: E — a histogram can be implemented by hand without np.histogram.
13. L7-10 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: a pooling layer has no trainable parameters; pattern recognition is the job of the convolutional layers. ⚠️ Trap: E — "a pooling layer learns weights" → "it has no trainable parameters" would make it TRUE.
14. L5-13 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: logistic regression can also be implemented with NumPy by computing the gradient yourself. ⚠️ Trap: E — "cannot be implemented with NumPy" → "can be implemented with NumPy" would make it TRUE.
15. L4-08 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Examples of hyperparameters (A true). Selected on the validation set (B true). A differentiable loss with respect to the hyperparameters cannot always be defined, so gradient descent cannot be used (C true). The standard search techniques (D true). The model parameters θ are learned on the training set by gradient descent (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "theta is also learned on the validation set" → "on the training set" would make it TRUE.

Mock Exam 2

1. L2-08 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: real data contains noise, so a small non-zero loss can still be a useful model. ⚠️ Trap: E — "the model is useless" → "the model is still useful" would make it TRUE.
2. L4-18 — Answer: C, D, E
The Lecture 4 summary slide states: included = identifying / handling under- and overfitting, hyperparameters, the validation set, the artificial neuron (perceptron) model. Excluded = polynomial interpolation, the differences between the biological neuron model and the artificial model. Therefore A and B are false. ⚠️ Trap: A — "polynomial interpolation is included" → "it is excluded" would make it TRUE. ⚠️ Trap: B — "the difference between the neuron models is included" → "it is excluded" would make it TRUE.
3. N-29 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: assignment through a mask really modifies the original array. ⚠️ Trap: E — "modifies a copy" → "modifies the original" would make it TRUE.
4. N-08 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: (32, 5) expands to (1, 32, 5), and combined with (32, 1, 5) the result is (32, 32, 5).
5. L7-12 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: the neurons of a convolutional layer only see a local region (their receptive field). ⚠️ Trap: E — "must receive the whole image at once" → "it only needs a local patch" would make it TRUE.
6. L1-05 — Answer: A, B, C, E
Possible unsupervised tasks: clustering, compression / dimensionality reduction, sample generation (A true). Autoencoder: minimize the normed difference between x and its reconstruction (B true). A compressed representation needs less storage and can be visualized in 2-3 dimensions (C true). No method is always better (D false). Segmentation, anomaly detection, inpainting and denoising are unsupervised applications (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — "always better than supervised" → "depends on the task" would make it TRUE.
7. N-02 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true (32x5=160). C true: an integer index removes that axis. D true: fancy (list) indexing keeps the axis. E false: axis 0 is an integer index, so it is removed and the shape is (5,).
8. L7-07 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: a convolutional layer has filters and biases as trainable parameters. ⚠️ Trap: E — "no trainable parameters at all" → "the filters are the trainable parameters" would make it TRUE.
9. L5-15 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: the loss of an MLP is differentiable, so gradient descent is used. ⚠️ Trap: E — "not differentiable, so gradient descent cannot be used" → "differentiable, so it can" would make it TRUE.
10. N-12 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true (reverse slicing is a view). B true. C true. D true (the transpose of a 1D array shares the data). E false: reshape(-1) returns a view when possible. ⚠️ Trap: E — "reshape(-1) returns a copy" → "it returns a view" would make it TRUE.
11. L3-14 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Binary classification gives a continuous probability estimate (A true). The 0.5 threshold (B true). Clipping out-of-range values to 0/1 is a known approach (C true). Assigning numbers to categories is problematic (D true). The sigmoid output is the probability of one (positive) class; forcing the two probabilities to sum to 1 is the job of softmax (multiclass) (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "forces the two probabilities to sum to 1" → "does not force it" would make it TRUE.
12. N-03 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true (axis 0 is a list of 2 elements, axis 1 is an integer). E false: axis 0 is removed and axis 1 has 5-1=4 elements, so the shape is (4,).
13. L3-16 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Regression → MSE (A true). Binary classification → BCE (B true). MSE for classification is non-convex, so local minima may appear (C true). BCE is convex, so the global optimum is guaranteed (D true). Regression uses MSE; cross-entropy is for classification (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "cross-entropy is standard for regression" → "MSE is standard" would make it TRUE.
14. L7-06 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: the filter slides over every position. ⚠️ Trap: E — "applied at only one position" → "applied at every position" would make it TRUE.
15. N-17 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true ([[0,1],[2,3]]). B true. C true (flattened). D true. E false: (2,2) and (2,2,1) give (2,2,2).

Mock Exam 3

1. L4-15 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Undesirable learning by a powerful model (A true). Memorizing even JPEG artifacts (B true). Enough labeled data for a huge model is hard to obtain (C true). Regularization, early stopping and other alternatives are needed (D true). Many parameters with little data easily leads to overfitting (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "does not overfit even with little data" → "easily overfits" would make it TRUE.
2. L2-03 — Answer: A, B, C, E
A true (slope of the chord). B true (slope of the tangent). C true. D false: x and y are known constants from the data. E true. ⚠️ Trap: D — "unknowns" → "constants" would make it TRUE.
3. N-26 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: with integer arguments the dtype of np.arange is an integer type (int64, ...).
4. L1-06 — Answer: A, B, C, D
The agent observes the state and influences the environment through actions (A true). A reward is given when predefined goals are met, and it may be negative (B true). The goal is to maximize the future total reward (C true). Sparse feedback creates the credit assignment problem (D true). No correct label is given at every step (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "a correct label at every step" → "sparse and delayed rewards" would make it TRUE.
5. L6-03 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Six steps: 1 symbolic derivation, 2 parameter initialization, 3 select an example/mini-batch, 4 forward pass (substitution), 5 backward pass (substitution), 6 update the parameters and go back to step 3 (A, B, C, D true). E reverses the order (false).
6. N-19 — Answer: A, B, C, D
The shape of an empty array is still a tuple (A true). size = 0 (B true). ndim = 3 (C true). Since there are no elements, "all" is vacuously true, so np.all is True (D true). np.any returns False (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "np.any of an empty array is True" → "it is False" would make it TRUE.
7. L6-06 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true (chain rule). C true. D true. E false: the derivatives of the elementary operations are still required.
8. N-23 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: column-wise aggregation uses axis=0; axis=1 is row-wise. ⚠️ Trap: E — "column-wise scaling uses axis=1" → "axis=0" would make it TRUE.
9. L3-06 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: the loss is always a scalar. ⚠️ Trap: E — "a vector" → "a scalar" would make it TRUE.
10. N-18 — Answer: A, B, C, D, E
There is only one zero -> 1 (A true). any -> True (B true). Each row contains a non-zero value, so the row-wise all is False, False (C true). All elements are >= 0, so all is True (D true). The non-zero elements are 1, 2 and 3, so count_nonzero(my_array) is 3, not 4 (E false).
11. N-34 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true (the (H,W) index plus the last axis 3). C true. D true. E false.
12. L1-09 — Answer: A, B, C, E
Detection: bounding box coordinates or pixel-level outlines (A true). Pose estimation: joint coordinates (B true). Sentiment analysis maps a sequence of characters or words to a sentiment category (C true). It is not always possible to provide all correct outputs (D false). Labeling is expensive while unlabeled data is abundant (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — "can always provide all possible outputs" → "cannot provide them all" would make it TRUE.
13. L7-18 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: no noise is applied during evaluation/prediction.
14. L3-05 — Answer: A, B, D, E
A true. B true. C false: the output is restricted, so it cannot solve arbitrary regression problems. D true. E true (the radiation example). ⚠️ Trap: C — "can be used for arbitrary regression" → "cannot, because the output is limited to 0-1" would make it TRUE.
15. N-01 — Answer: A, D
shape is always a tuple, so (5, 6) is possible but the integer 12 is not (A true, B false). dtype is a data type (int64, float32, ...) and cannot be a tuple (C false). size is the total number of elements, an integer (D true). ndim is the number of dimensions, an integer, so it cannot be a tuple (E false). ⚠️ Trap: B — "shape can be the integer 12" → "shape is a tuple" would make it TRUE. ⚠️ Trap: C — "dtype can be (5, 6)" → "dtype is a data type" would make it TRUE. ⚠️ Trap: E — "ndim can be (5, 6)" → "ndim is an integer" would make it TRUE.

Mock Exam 4

1. N-25 — Answer: A, B, C, D
A true. B true. C true. D true. E false: fancy indexing returns a copy, so the original does not change. ⚠️ Trap: E — "always changes the original" → "it is a copy, so the original is unchanged" would make it TRUE.
2. N-15 — Answer: A, B, C, D
In reshape, -1 can be specified only once -> ValueError (A true). [:, None] gives (4,1) and [None, :] gives (1,4) (B, C true). np.dot follows the matrix multiplication rule, so (32,5) with (5,1) -> (32,1) (D true). (32,5) and (32,) do not align (5 vs 32), so a ValueError is raised (E false).

Practice Set 1

1. S1-12 — Answer: A, B, C, D
With λ = 0 the penalty vanishes (A true). A large λ shrinks the weights toward 0 and can cause underfitting (B true). By convention the bias θ₀ is not penalized (C true). Limiting the weight magnitude reduces variance and overfitting (D true).
2. S2-21 — Answer: A, B, C, D, E
60,000 / 100 = 600 steps (A true). Using the whole set gives 1 step per epoch (B true). Large batches need more activation/gradient memory (C true). Small batches are cheap but noisy (D true). Adam and RMSprop are adaptive-learning-rate variants of SGD (E true).
3. S2-33 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Mixing ints and floats promotes to float (A true). / is true division (B true). // is floor division (C true). bools sum as True=1 (D true). Adding a float promotes to float (E false).
4. S2-15 — Answer: A, B, C, D
By convention the bias θ₀ is not regularized, so the sum starts at j = 1 (A true, E false). With λ = 0 the penalty vanishes (B true). A huge λ shrinks the weights toward 0 and causes underfitting (C true). Large weights are penalized (D true). ⚠️ Trap: E — "the sum includes θ₀" → "θ₀ is excluded" would make it TRUE.
5. S1-23 — Answer: A, B, C, E
Noise is used only while training as a regularizer (A true). Dropout randomly zeroes some activations (B true). While training, some units are off, so the loss looks larger (C true). At evaluation time all units are used (D false). Augmentation creates extra training examples (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — "also applied at test time" → "not applied at test time" would make it TRUE.
6. S2-30 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Weight sharing makes conv layers translation equivariant (A true). Pooling plus hierarchy gives (ideal) translation invariance (B true). An MLP needs separate weights per location (C true). 1D/2D/3D convolutions apply to audio, time series and medical images (D true, E false).
7. S2-35 — Answer: A, B, C, E

8. S2-24 — Answer: A, B, C, D

10. S1-08 — Answer: A, B, C, D

11. S2-27 — Answer: A, B, C

1. S1-07 — Answer: A, B, D

4. S1-26 — Answer: A, C

12. S2-15 — Answer: A, B, C, D
By convention the bias θ₀ is not regularized, so the sum starts at j = 1 (A true, E false). With λ = 0 the penalty vanishes (B true). A huge λ shrinks the weights toward 0 and causes underfitting (C true). Large weights are penalized (D true). ⚠️ Trap: E — "the sum includes θ₀" → "θ₀ is excluded" would make it TRUE.
13. S2-24 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Without nonlinearity the network is only linear, so intermediate nonlinearities are essential (A true, B true). The output activation depends on the task (C true). ReLU = max(0, z) is cheap (D true). ReLU outputs are ≥ 0 (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "any real number" → "≥ 0" would make it TRUE.
14. S1-02 — Answer: A, C
A few changed pixels can flip the estimate (A true). Formal verification of network decisions is very hard (B false). Learning the background is a common shortcut (C true). Attacks work even without knowing the architecture (D false). ⚠️ Trap: D — "always need the architecture and parameters" → "black-box attacks work too" would make it TRUE.
15. S2-30 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Weight sharing makes conv layers translation equivariant (A true). Pooling plus hierarchy gives (ideal) translation invariance (B true). An MLP needs separate weights per location (C true). 1D/2D/3D convolutions apply to audio, time series and medical images (D true, E false).

Final Mock Exam

1. M-01 — Answer: A, B, D, E
θ₀ is the bias parameter (a scalar) (A true). Input features such as age live in x (B true, C false). With no output activation, y can be any real number (D true). Two inputs give a plane (E true). One input gives a line, not a parabola (F false). ⚠️ Trap: F — "a parabola" → "a straight line" would make it TRUE.
2. M-02 — Answer: A, C, D
Regression and classification need labels, so they are supervised (A true). Clustering is unsupervised (B false). Dimensionality reduction and denoising need no labels (C true). RL feedback is a reward, often sparse and delayed (D true). Linear regression is supervised (E false). ⚠️ Trap: B — "needs labeled data" → "needs no labels" would make it TRUE. ⚠️ Trap: E — "unsupervised" → "supervised" would make it TRUE.
3. M-03 — Answer: A, B, C, D
Binary classification calls for logistic regression (A true). Three inputs plus the dummy give 4 parameters (B true). X is m×(n+1) = 150×4 (C true). The sigmoid output is in (0,1) (D true). The loss should be binary cross-entropy; MSE becomes non-convex here (E false).
4. M-04 — Answer: A, B, C, E
12·5 + 5 = 65 (A true). The second layer has 5·1 + 1 = 6, so the total is 71 (B true). A binary output uses a sigmoid (C true). Omitting g₁ makes the whole network linear (D false). Binary classification uses BCE (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — "can be omitted" → "cannot be omitted" would make it TRUE.
5. M-05 — Answer: A, B, C, E
Falling training loss with rising validation loss means overfitting (A true). Early stopping stops near the minimum of the validation loss (B true). More training data improves generalization (C true). The test-set size does not change overfitting (D false). More parameters increase capacity and overfitting (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — the test-set size does not change overfitting itself.
6. M-06 — Answer: A, B, C, E, F
X is m×(n+1) = 300×7 (A true). There is one label per example, so y is 300×1 (B true). θ has n+1 = 7 elements (C true). I is (n+1)×(n+1) = 7×7, not 300×300 (D false, E true). The labels (cholesterol levels) are in y (F true).
7. M-07 — Answer: A, B, D
Scaling aligns the magnitudes of the inputs (A true). Very different scales make the loss surface elongated and slow down convergence (B true). Min-max scaling depends on the min/max, so it is MORE sensitive to outliers (C false). In x′ = (x − μ)/σ, μ is the mean and σ the standard deviation (D true). Standardized values are not limited to [0,1] (E false). ⚠️ Trap: C — "more robust" → "more sensitive" would make it TRUE. ⚠️ Trap: E — "always in [0, 1]" → "not limited to [0, 1]" would make it TRUE.
8. M-08 — Answer: A, B, C, D
θ (W, b) is optimized by gradient descent (A true). Hyperparameters are not differentiable and are searched (B true). Larger batches need more activation/gradient memory (C true). Fewer samples give a higher-variance (noisier) estimate (D true). A larger batch gives LESS noise (lower variance) (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "higher-variance" → "lower-variance" would make it TRUE.
9. M-09 — Answer: A, C, D, E
The square node gives grad_b = grad_c·2b = 3·2·(−2) = −12 (A true, B false). The addition node passes the gradient through (C true). The multiplication node swaps the other input: grad_a = grad_c·b = 15 and grad_b = grad_c·a = 6 (D and E true). ⚠️ Trap: B — "12" → "−12" would make it TRUE.
10. M-10 — Answer: A, C, D, E, F
A basic slice keeps the axis, giving (32,2) (A true). my_array[-1] selects the last row, so it is (5,), not (1,5) (B false). ::2 picks 16 rows (C true). [[3],[1]] indexes two axes with integer arrays, giving (1,) (D true). Three column indices give (32,3) (E true). None inserts a new axis, giving (1,32,5) (F true).
11. M-11 — Answer: C, E
(32,5)+(5,) broadcasts along the columns (A false). (32,5)*(32,1) also broadcasts (B false). (32,5)+(1,32) mismatches the trailing axes 5 vs 32 (C true). mean(axis=0) is (5,) so it works (D false). mean(axis=1) is (32,) which does not match the trailing 5 (E true). ⚠️ Trap: D — mean(axis=0) is (5,) so it works — axis=1 would raise.
12. M-12 — Answer: A, B, C, D, E
Reshaping 6 elements to (3, -1) gives (3,2) (A true). The matrix is [[0,1],[2,3],[4,5]] and the axis=0 sum is [0+2+4, 1+3+5] = [6,9] (B true). The odd elements are [1,3,5] (C true). The per-row count above 2 is 0, 1, 2 (D true). All elements are ≥ 0 (E true). Since 5 > 4, np.any(my_array > 4) is True, not False (F false). ⚠️ Trap: F — "is False" → "is True" would make it TRUE.
13. M-13 — Answer: A, B, C
Transpose (.T) returns a view (A true). A single integer index also returns a view (B true). A basic slice [:, 1:] is a view (C true). Integer-array indexing [:, [0, 2]] returns a copy (D false). flatten() returns a copy (E false). Arithmetic (+ 0) creates a new array (F false). ⚠️ Trap: E — flatten() makes a copy — a view such as "my_array.T" would make it TRUE.
14. M-14 — Answer: A, B, C, E
unique gives the sorted distinct values [0,1,2] (A true). bincount stores the count of value i at index i: [2,1,3] (B true). There are three 2s (C true). v[v != 0] keeps the non-zeros: [2,2,1,2], not [2,1,2] (D false). v != 0 is a boolean array whose unique values are [False,True] (E true). The length of bincount is max+1 = 3 (F false). ⚠️ Trap: D — v[v != 0] is [2, 2, 1, 2].
15. M-15 — Answer: A, B, C, E, F
(4,3)+(3,) broadcasts along the columns giving (4,3) (A true). (4,1,3)+(3,) gives (4,1,3) (B true). (4,1,3)+(1,4,3) gives (4,4,3) (C true). sum(axis=1) collapses the columns, so the shape is (4,), not (3,) (D false). mean(axis=0, keepdims=True) is (1,3) (E true). (4,3) @ (3,) gives (4,) (F true). ⚠️ Trap: D — sum(axis=1) is (4,) — "(3,)" → "(4,)" would make it TRUE.

Final Mock Exam 2 (formulas)

1. M2-01 — Answer: A, B, D, F
X is 120×5, so XᵀX is 5×5 (A true). Xᵀy has 5 elements (B true). I is (n+1)×(n+1) = 5×5, not 120×120 (C false). With λ = 0 we get the plain normal equation (D true). θ has n+1 = 5 elements (E false). There is one label per example, so y has 120 elements (F true). ⚠️ Trap: C — "120×120" → "5×5" would make it TRUE.
2. M2-02 — Answer: A, B, C, F
The errors are 1, −1, −3, so the sum of squares is 1+1+9 = 11 (B true). J = 11/(2·3) = 11/6 (A true, D false). With 1/m it would be 11/3 (C true). A sum of squares is never negative (E false). ∂J/∂ŷ⁽¹⁾ = (1/(2m))·2·(ŷ−y) = (ŷ−y)/m = 1/3 (F true). ⚠️ Trap: E — "can be negative" → "is always ≥ 0" would make it TRUE.
3. M2-03 — Answer: A, B, C, E
The errors are [[0, 2], [2, 0]] and the squares sum to 4+4 = 8 (B true). 2mk = 2·2·2 = 8 (C true). J = 8/8 = 1 (A true, D false). With several outputs the loss is also averaged over the elements (E true). J is a scalar (F false). ⚠️ Trap: F — "a vector" → "a scalar" would make it TRUE.
4. M2-04 — Answer: A, B, C, D, F
With y = 1, L = −ln ŷ = −ln 0.8 ≈ 0.22 (A true). With y = 0, L = −ln(1 − ŷ) = −ln 0.8 ≈ 0.22 (B true). y = 1 and ŷ = 0.5 give 0.69 (C true). As ŷ → 0, −ln ŷ → ∞ (D true). For y = 0 the meaningful term is the second one, (1 − y)·ln(1 − ŷ) (E false). Sigmoid + BCE is convex (F true).
5. M2-05 — Answer: A, B, C, D
With a one-hot label only the true-class term survives: L = −ln 0.7 ≈ 0.36 (A true, B true). With a uniform estimate L = −ln(1/3) = ln 3 ≈ 1.10 (C true). A softmax output is a probability distribution, so it sums to 1 (D true). y is the one-hot label; the softmax output is ŷ (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "y is the softmax output" → "ŷ is the softmax output" would make it TRUE.
6. M2-06 — Answer: A, B, C, D
W₁ is (hidden, input) = 6×10 (A true). W₂ is (output, hidden) = 3×6 (B true). b₂ has one element per output neuron, so it is in ℝ³ (C true). 6·10+6 + 3·6+3 = 66+21 = 87 (D true). W₁x + b₁ lives in the hidden space ℝ⁶ (E false). A multi-class output uses softmax (F false).
7. M2-07 — Answer: A, B, C, D
∂J/∂θ = 2(θ−3) = −4 (A true). θ_new = 1 − 0.25·(−4) = 2 (B true). J(1) = 4 and J(2) = 1 (C true). With α = 1.5, θ_new = 1 + 6 = 7 and J(7) = 16, so the loss grows (D true). Adding the gradient increases the loss (E false).
8. M2-08 — Answer: A, B, C, D
f′(0) = 0.5·0.5 = 0.25 (A true). The derivative of the sigmoid is f(1−f) (B true). f′(ln 3) = 0.75·0.25 = 0.1875 = 3/16 (C true). e⁻ˣ/(1+e⁻ˣ)² is an equivalent form of f′ (D true). The maximum of f′ is 0.25 at x = 0 (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "the maximum is 1" → "the maximum is 0.25" would make it TRUE.
9. M2-09 — Answer: A, B, C, D, E
q = 2, ŷ = 1 and z = 2, so J = 4 (A true). ∂J/∂z = 2z = 4, hence ∂J/∂ŷ = −4 and ∂J/∂b = −4 (B true). ∂J/∂w = x·(−4) = −4 (C true). ∂J/∂x = w·(−4) = −8 (D true). With α = 0.5: w_new = 2 + 2 = 4 and b_new = −1 + 2 = 1 (E true). The new ŷ = 5 gives z = −2 and J = 4 again: the step is too large (overshoot), so F is false. ⚠️ Trap: F — "the loss is 0" → "the loss stays 4 (overshoot)" would make it TRUE.
10. M2-10 — Answer: A, B, D, E, F
The errors are [1, −1, −3] and the squares sum to 11 (A true). mean of squares / 2 = (11/3)/2 ≈ 1.83 (B true). np.mean(yp−y) = −1, so its square is 1, not 11/3 (this is a classic bug) (C false). (3,) − (3,1) broadcasts to (3, 3) (D true). yp > y is [T, F, F], so the sum is 1 (E true). The argmax of |error| = [1,1,3] is 2 (F true). ⚠️ Trap: C — np.mean(d)**2 is 1 — "np.mean(d**2)" would make it TRUE.
11. M2-11 — Answer: A, B, C, E
(4,100)@(100,4) gives (4,4) (A true). (4,100)@(100,) gives (4,) (B true). The inverse of (4,4)+(4,4) is (4,4) (C true). (4,4) + (100,100) does not broadcast and raises a ValueError (D false). (4,4)@(4,) gives (4,) (E true).
12. M2-12 — Answer: A, B, C, D
z = [0, ln 3] gives h = [0.5, 0.75] (A true). The mean BCE is (0.693 + 1.386)/2 ≈ 1.04 (B true). (2,2)@(2,)/2 gives (2,) (C true). h − y = [−0.5, 0.75], so Xᵀ(h−y) = [0.25, 0.75] and /2 = [0.125, 0.375] (D true). h − y is [−0.5, 0.75], not [0.5, 0.75] (E false). ⚠️ Trap: E — "h - y equals [0.5, 0.75]" → "[-0.5, 0.75]" would make it TRUE.
13. M2-13 — Answer: A, B, C, D, F
The column means are [2.5, 25] (A true). Population std: √1.25 ≈ 1.118 for the first column and √125 ≈ 11.18 for the second (B true). (1−2.5)/1.118 ≈ −1.34 (C true). The second value of min-max scaling is (2−1)/3 = 1/3 (D true). mean(axis=1) averages each row, so its shape is (4,) (E false). max−min = [3, 30] (F true). ⚠️ Trap: E — mean(axis=1) is (4,) — "(2,)" → "(4,)" would make it TRUE.
14. M2-14 — Answer: A, B, D, E
e = [[1,1],[2,1]] with row sums [2,3], so P[1] = [2/3, 1/3] ≈ [0.667, 0.333] (A true). Each row of the softmax output sums to 1 (B true). e / e.sum(axis=1) is (2,2)/(2,) and here the trailing dimension happens to be 2, so it does NOT raise — it silently divides each column by the wrong number (C false). keepdims gives (2,1) (D true). The first element is the largest in both rows, so the argmax is [0, 0] (E true). ⚠️ Trap: C — the trailing dimension happens to be 2, so it does not raise — use keepdims=True.
15. M2-15 — Answer: A, B, C, D, F
X@θ−y = [−1,−3,−5], so Xᵀ(…) = [−9,−13] and /3 = [−3, −4.33] (A true). θ_new = [0,0] − 0.1·[−3,−4.33] = [0.3, 0.433] (B true). The initial loss is (1+9+25)/(2·3) = 35/6 ≈ 5.83 (C true). grad has shape (2,) (D true). Adding the gradient increases the loss (E false). (3,2)@(2,) gives (3,) (F true). ⚠️ Trap: E — "adding decreases the loss" → "subtracting decreases the loss" would make it TRUE.
